 &lt;/footer&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;</content:encoded>
					
		
		
			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Evaluate skill-equipped agents with Strands Evals and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/evaluate-skill-equipped-agents-with-strands-evals-and-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Sangmin Woo]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 22 Sep 2026 17:18:13 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">aa00d9c221ad9292cfbcc5630acd0f904e2648ad</guid>

					<description>Skills let you encode domain-specific procedures as reusable, portable instructions for agents, but a fluent answer doesn't prove the agent picked the right skill or followed it. Learn how to measure skill selection and instruction following with Strands Evals and Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;General-purpose agents handle a broad range of tasks, but you still need them to follow the procedures that run your business: compliance checks, document-processing workflows, escalation policies, engineering conventions. Encoding all of that in one system prompt or in application logic gets hard to maintain and update. Skills are a modular alternative. A skill is a reusable set of instructions, usually stored in a &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; file, that teaches an agent a domain-specific task like redacting a contract, reconciling an invoice, or following a team’s pull-request conventions. Because skills follow the open Agent Skills standard, they are portable across compatible harnesses, and the agent loads only the skill it needs at runtime instead of carrying every procedure in its core instructions.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A skill packages one or more tools with the context an agent needs to use them correctly:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Instructions&lt;/strong&gt;: Domain-specific guidance and constraints injected into the agent’s context.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool bindings&lt;/strong&gt;: The APIs, Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, or local commands the skill depends on.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge&lt;/strong&gt;: Reference material and worked examples.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workflow&lt;/strong&gt;: The multi-step procedure or decision logic the skill follows.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Guardrails&lt;/strong&gt;: Format requirements, scope limits, and validation rules.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This modular approach helps teams specialize agents faster, reuse proven procedures across agents and workflows, keep behavior consistent, and update domain-specific guidance without fine-tuning the underlying model or rewriting the agent’s core logic.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;This skill composability in agents introduces two failure modes that general output-quality metrics can miss: the agent invokes a skill that is not appropriate for the task and the agent invokes the right skill but skips or only partially follows its instructions. Both failures can produce a fluent, plausible response without having used your pre-determined domain knowledge. An evaluation therefore cannot examine the final response alone.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To make these failures measurable, &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/evals-sdk/quickstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Evals SDK&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, add skill-focused evaluators:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Skill Selection Accuracy determines whether each invoked skill was an appropriate choice for the task. It returns a binary result for each invoked skill.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Skill Instruction Following determines how fully the agent followed an invoked skill’s instructions. It returns a five-level rating grounded in evidence for each prescribed step.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Additionally on Strands Evals, Skill Invoked is a deterministic check of whether a named skill has been loaded successfully.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you will learn how to evaluate skill selection and instruction following from a recorded trajectory in Strands Evals, add deterministic routing checks to a test suite, evaluate skill behavior from OpenTelemetry traces with AgentCore Evaluations, and interpret per-skill results to choose the right fix, all through the AgentCore CLI.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="understand-what-each-evaluator-measures"&gt;Understand what each evaluator measures&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;An agent receives a task and a catalog of skills, chooses a skill, loads it, and acts. The run is recorded as a trajectory in Strands Evals or an OpenTelemetry trace in your observability layer. This record can now be used for all three skill evaluators. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/skill-evaluators.html#skill-evaluators-ssa" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Skill Selection Accuracy&lt;/a&gt; checks whether each invoked skill fits the task and whether the agent invoked the correct skill. The following figure shows how the agent chooses a skill from the 1:n skills provided to it. Skill Selection Accuracy then scores whether the selected skill is the correct one for the task. You can find the prompt template and the rubric of this evaluator &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/prompt-templates-builtin.html#skill-selection-accuracy" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;in the prompt template documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-1.png" alt="Diagram of an agent selecting one skill from a catalog of skills, with Skill Selection Accuracy scoring whether that choice fits the task" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Skill Selection Accuracy checks whether the agent chose an appropriate skill for the task&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/skill-evaluators.html#skill-evaluators-sif" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Skill Instruction Following&lt;/a&gt; asks how fully the agent followed that skill’s prescribed steps. You can find the prompt template and the rubric for this evaluator &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/prompt-templates-builtin.html#skill-instruction-following" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;in the prompt template documentation&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-2.png" alt="Diagram of Skill Instruction Following checking how completely the agent executed the invoked skill’s prescribed steps" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Skill Instruction Following measures how completely the agent followed the skill’s steps&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; is deterministic. It calls no model and is specific to Strands Evals.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;An overview of these skill evaluators is demonstrated diagrammatically in the following figure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21794-3.png" alt="Overview diagram of the three skill evaluators: Skill Selection Accuracy, Skill Instruction Following, and Skill Invoked" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Overview of the three skill evaluators&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Consider an &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/06-observe-evaluate-optimize-your-agent/02-evaluate/skills-evaluation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HR assistant agent&lt;/a&gt; with skills for paid time off (PTO) planning and discussing employee benefits. An employee asks about their dental and vision benefits. If the agent invokes the benefits skill, it may produce a more polished response. If the tool call succeeded but the agent chose the wrong playbook, Skill Selection Accuracy isolates that routing decision.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Now suppose the agent correctly invokes the PTO-planning skill for a related request. The skill instructs the agent to identify the &lt;code&gt;employee_id&lt;/code&gt;, check the PTO balance, check the rollover rules against the latest HR policy, and then submit a PTO request if the conditions allow. If the agent checks the PTO balance but skips the rollover rules, the agent might still return a plausible response while violating the prescribed process. Skill Instruction Following isolates that execution failure and identifies the skipped step.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The failures require different fixes. An inappropriate selection often points to overlapping or ambiguous skill descriptions. Incomplete instruction following might call for clearer steps, a different skill structure, or a more capable agent model.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Evaluator&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Availability&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Score&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Question answered&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skill Selection Accuracy&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Strands Evals and AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Binary, per invoked skill&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Was invoking this skill appropriate for the task?&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skill Instruction Following&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Strands Evals and AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Five levels, per invoked skill&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;How fully did the agent follow this skill’s prescribed steps?&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Skill Invoked&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Strands Evals&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Binary, deterministic&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Was this named skill successfully loaded?&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Because judge-based evaluators return per-invoked-skill results, multi-skill runs remain diagnosable: you can identify which selection or instruction-following result lowered the aggregate score. If no skill is invoked, the judge-based evaluators don’t produce a score. Pair them with &lt;code&gt;SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; when a regression test has a known routing requirement.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.10 or later.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with Amazon Bedrock access, and credentials with &lt;code&gt;InvokeModel&lt;/code&gt; permission for the judge model.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;To follow the Strands Evals section, install the SDKs:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;pip install strands-agents-evals strands-agents&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;You also need a recorded agent run. The skill evaluators accept either a Strands Evals Session or a raw message list as the trajectory. At launch, skill extraction recognizes signals from the Strands AgentSkills plugin, Claude Code, Claude Agent SDK, OpenAI Agents SDK, Codex, Gemini CLI, OpenHands, Google ADK, and generic &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; file reads.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;To follow the AgentCore Evaluations section, you need:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An agent hosted on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime or elsewhere. We will use the example of the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/main/01-features/06-observe-evaluate-optimize-your-agent/02-evaluate/utils/hr_assistant_agent.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;HR assistant agent&lt;/a&gt; which you can deploy in your account.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Observability enabled for that agent, so it delivers telemetry to Amazon CloudWatch.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Transaction Search enabled in CloudWatch.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The examples in this post use the AgentCore CLI:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;npm install -g @aws/agentcore&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="evaluate-a-recorded-trajectory-with-strands-evals"&gt;Evaluate a recorded trajectory with Strands Evals&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Strands Evals is useful when you control the test cases and can rerun the agent during development or continuous integration. Check out the complete Strands evals code sample created for the HR assistant agent in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/blob/main/01-features/06-observe-evaluate-optimize-your-agent/02-evaluate/skills-evaluation/inner_loop_eval.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;complete code sample&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="define-the-case-and-evaluators"&gt;1. Define the case and evaluators&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands_evals import Case, Experiment
from strands_evals.evaluators import (
    SkillInstructionFollowingEvaluator,
    SkillInvoked,
    SkillSelectionAccuracyEvaluator,
)

case = Case(
    name="q3-revenue-tables",
    input="Summarize the revenue tables in q3-report.pdf",
)

evaluators = [
    SkillSelectionAccuracyEvaluator(),
    SkillInstructionFollowingEvaluator(),
    SkillInvoked(skill_name="pdf-table-extraction"),
]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="run-the-agent-and-capture-its-trajectory"&gt;2. Run the agent and capture its trajectory&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The skill evaluators read the run’s trajectory. TracedHandler collects the agent’s spans and attaches them as the trajectory:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;from strands import Agent
from strands_evals import TracedHandler, eval_task

@eval_task(TracedHandler())
def task_function():
    return Agent(...) # your skill-equipped agent

experiment = Experiment(cases=[case], evaluators=evaluators)
report = experiment.run_evaluations(task_function)
report.run_display()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="interpret-the-report"&gt;3. Interpret the report&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Suppose the agent loaded &lt;code&gt;pdf-table-extraction&lt;/code&gt;, ran &lt;code&gt;pdftotext -layout&lt;/code&gt;, but never opened the extracted file or located the table boundaries. A simplified report:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;SkillSelectionAccuracyEvaluator: score=1.00, pass=True
pdf-table-extraction: The skill directly matches the request.
SkillInstructionFollowingEvaluator: score=0.50, pass=False
pdf-table-extraction: The extraction phase was completed,
the table-boundary phase was skipped.
Steps:
- Extract text with layout preservation: covered
- Locate table boundaries: skipped
- Summarize each table's headline figure: partial
SkillInvoked: score=1.00, pass=True
skill 'pdf-table-extraction' was invoked&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Skill Instruction Following uses five ratings: Fully Followed (1.0), Mostly Followed (0.75), Partially Followed (0.5), Minimally Followed (0.25), and Not Followed (0.0). It passes at Mostly Followed or better.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="turn-the-checks-into-a-deployment-gate"&gt;Turn the checks into a deployment gate&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Add &lt;code&gt;SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; for every critical skill that a specific regression case must invoke. Because it doesn’t call a model, it is a fast routing assertion.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Gate the build on &lt;code&gt;report.test_passes&lt;/code&gt;: &lt;code&gt;if not all(report.test_passes): raise SystemExit(1)&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use aggregate scores to track broader trends, but calibrate the threshold on your own cases before enforcing it.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="evaluate-production-traces-with-agentcore-evaluations"&gt;Evaluate production traces with AgentCore Evaluations&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Evaluations works directly with existing OpenTelemetry traces, supporting on-demand evaluation, batch processing of stored sessions, and continuous sampling of live traffic. Telemetry is organized into sessions, traces, and spans. Because skill evaluators operate at the tool-call level, each result includes a &lt;code&gt;spanContext&lt;/code&gt; with the &lt;code&gt;sessionId&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;traceId&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;spanId&lt;/code&gt; of the recognized skill invocation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A skill invocation is recognized through either a &lt;code&gt;SKILL.md&lt;/code&gt; filesystem read, which works across frameworks, or a native skill-loading tool in Strands Agents, LangGraph Deep Agents, Google ADK, or the Claude Agent SDK.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="trace-placeholders-for-custom-evaluators"&gt;Trace placeholders for custom evaluators&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore Evaluations includes two built-in judge-based evaluators for skills. To score something the built-ins don’t cover, create a custom evaluator at the &lt;code&gt;TOOL_CALL&lt;/code&gt; level. Tool-level templates can reference skill placeholders:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Placeholder&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Contents&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{invoked_skill}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Name of the skill loaded on this tool call&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{skill_content}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The loaded SKILL.md body&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{available_skills}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The catalog offered at runtime, or “(not recorded by this harness)”&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{user_message}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The request that triggered the invocation&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;{context}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;The conversation record&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For example, a template that checks one specific property of a skill run:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;## Skill instructions

{skill_content}

## Conversation record

{context}

## Evaluation Question

Did the agent complete every numbered step in the skill instructions above, in the order given? Answer Yes or No.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The placeholders you reference also decide when the evaluator runs. A template containing &lt;code&gt;{invoked_skill}&lt;/code&gt; runs only on skill-invocation spans, and one containing &lt;code&gt;{skill_content}&lt;/code&gt; additionally requires the loaded body.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following commands target the separate skill-enabled runtime. Substitute the runtime name from skills-evaluation/&lt;code&gt;agent_config.json&lt;/code&gt; (&lt;code&gt;agent_id&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;agent_arn&lt;/code&gt;) for &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;skill-runtime&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;. &lt;code&gt;Strands.SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; is client-side only and has no CLI equivalent.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="run-an-on-demand-evaluation"&gt;Run an on-demand evaluation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use on-demand evaluation to investigate a session, validate a recent change, or evaluate staged traffic:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;agentcore run eval \
  --runtime &amp;lt;skill-runtime&amp;gt; \
  --evaluator Builtin.SkillSelectionAccuracy Builtin.SkillInstructionFollowing \
  --session-id &amp;lt;session-id&amp;gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="run-a-batch-evaluation"&gt;Run a batch evaluation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use batch evaluation to score many stored sessions at once, for example to establish a baseline before a skill-catalog change:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;agentcore run batch-evaluation \
  --runtime &amp;lt;skill-runtime&amp;gt; \
  --evaluator Builtin.SkillSelectionAccuracy Builtin.SkillInstructionFollowing&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="configure-continuous-online-evaluation"&gt;Configure continuous online evaluation&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Online evaluation samples live traffic so you can detect behavior that a curated test set didn’t anticipate:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;agentcore add online-eval \
  --name HRSkillsProductionEval \
  --runtime &amp;lt;skill-runtime&amp;gt; \
  --evaluator Builtin.SkillSelectionAccuracy Builtin.SkillInstructionFollowing \
  --sampling-rate 100 \
  --enable-on-create&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Then deploy to provision the online evaluation configuration:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Continuous evaluation is particularly useful for detecting catalog drift (when a new skill overlaps with an existing description), unanticipated phrasing (when real requests differ from curated test prompts), and long-session failures (when instruction following degrades as context grows).&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="best-practices"&gt;Best practices&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When you’re evaluating agent skills, the first thing to internalize is that routing and execution are two different failure modes, and your evaluation strategy needs to separate them cleanly. If you see a high selection score but a low instruction-following score, that indicates the router picked the correct skill but it was not completely executed. The opposite pattern means the skill would have worked fine if only it had been invoked. Running these two evaluations together, rather than collapsing them into one pass/fail number, is what lets you tell those two stories apart.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before you build anything custom, start with built-in evaluators to establish a baseline, so any custom logic you add afterward can cover the gap the baseline actually missed. For requirements where you already know the correct routing behavior, don’t rely on a judge model to catch it. Add a deterministic &lt;code&gt;SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; assertion for every skill that must fire.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After you’re running evaluations, resist the urge to only look at the aggregate score. Per-step evidence is where the real diagnosis happens. It tells you whether an instruction was fully covered, partially completed, or skipped outright, and that level of detail is what turns a failing eval into an actionable fix. This is also why you should evaluate at every lifecycle stage instead of waiting for the final output. A failure at the end doesn’t tell you whether the router sent the request to the wrong place or the right skill executed poorly, and you need both signals to know what to fix.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Skill-level and end-to-end evaluation should be paired, because a skill can execute perfectly and still be the wrong skill for the request in front of it. You will reduce a lot of this ambiguity upstream by writing skill descriptions that are genuinely discriminative. The same logic applies to scope of a skill. A skill built to handle six unrelated functions doesn’t have a single definition of correct behavior, which makes it nearly impossible to evaluate consistently.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;State clearly that only the path actually taken in a given run should be evaluated, otherwise untaken branches get miscounted as skipped steps and quietly corrupt your pass rates. And before you trust any of these scores, validate that your extraction pipeline is actually working against live traces.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;As you scale this across tools, thresholds may not transfer cleanly. Each evaluation surface needs to be calibrated on its own terms, since a passing score on Strands Evals and a passing score on AgentCore Evaluations aren’t guaranteed to mean the same thing. And ultimately, none of this should live outside your deployment pipeline. Skill quality regressions need to block a release the same way a failing unit test would, or the evaluation work you’ve done up to that point isn’t actually protecting production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Skills make agents inexpensive to specialize, but a plausible final answer does not prove that the agent selected the right procedure or followed it. Skill Selection Accuracy and Skill Instruction Following separate those failure modes and return evidence for each invoked skill. In Strands Evals, &lt;code&gt;SkillInvoked&lt;/code&gt; adds a deterministic guard for known routing requirements. In AgentCore Evaluations, the two judge-based metrics can run on demand for a specific session, as a batch over stored sessions, or continuously over sampled traffic.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Use Strands Evals when you have test cases and recorded trajectories you can rerun. Use AgentCore Evaluations when you want to evaluate OpenTelemetry traces from staged or live agents. Many teams will use both: deterministic and judge-based gates before deployment, followed by trace-based monitoring in production.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/evals-sdk/quickstart/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Evals quickstart&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents/evals" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Evals repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Evaluations developer guide&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/evaluating-ai-agents-a-production-blueprint-with-strands-and-agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Evaluating AI agents blog post&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How Reactiv automates mobile commerce 80% faster with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-reactiv-automates-mobile-commerce-80-faster-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Adam Gibicar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 22 Sep 2026 15:46:07 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">b9998174e9c0e9055ac7cefe40c88652e9606651</guid>

					<description>Reactiv used Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to build a multi-agent AI Scheduler that autonomously refreshes Shopify merchants' mobile apps on a schedule, reducing merchant configuration time by 80% and getting to production 33% faster.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.reactiv.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Reactiv&lt;/a&gt; offers a mobile commerce product that helps Shopify merchants launch and manage native mobile apps, where shoppers convert at 2–4 times the rate of web visitors. For these merchants, a stale homepage or a missed promotional window costs real revenue. Yet keeping an app fresh requires constant manual work: choosing which products to feature, rearranging sections, generating new assets, and publishing updates on time. Most merchants don’t have the bandwidth to do this every week. Reactiv used Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to automate these updates, reducing merchant configuration time by 80 percent and getting to production 33 percent faster.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reactiv set out to solve this with an AI Scheduler that updates merchant apps autonomously on a schedule. A merchant describes what they want in natural language, such as “Refresh my homepage with best sellers every Monday at 9 AM,” and the system handles the rest. They built it as a three-agent system on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; with the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt; SDK and had it in production within weeks. They then unified their interactive and scheduled agents onto a single stack, achieving shared memory across both modes.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through the architecture behind those results, why Reactiv chose Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and how their product has evolved since launch.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-content-that-doesnt-update-itself"&gt;The challenge: Content that doesn’t update itself&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reactiv builds native iOS and Android apps for Shopify merchants. Their tools include a low-code app builder, analytics dashboards, and AI-powered features. Before the AI Scheduler, Reactiv had already built a conversational AI builder. Merchants could chat with an AI assistant in the Reactiv dashboard to modify their app in real time.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That system worked for interactive use, but Reactiv’s merchants asked for something different: autonomous updates that happen on a schedule without any manual intervention. Building this required capabilities that the existing architecture didn’t support:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-agent orchestration&lt;/strong&gt;. The scheduled task needed a supervisor to classify intent, an analytics agent to query merchant data, and a builder agent to generate configurations. Reactiv needed a managed runtime purpose-built for directed graphs of specialized agents, which led them to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Persistent memory&lt;/strong&gt;. Every session started from scratch. The agent had no way to remember what a merchant preferred across runs or learn from past approvals.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native Model Context Protocol (MCP) support&lt;/strong&gt;. Reactiv’s configuration schema server (the &lt;em&gt;Config MCP&lt;/em&gt;) ran on Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) with a custom Amazon Cognito authentication layer and manual JSON-RPC handshakes on every invocation.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool definition overhead&lt;/strong&gt;. Each tool required an OpenAPI specification, AWS Lambda wiring, and action-group mapping. Approximately 100 spec files were maintained across two locations.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reactiv needed a solution that could host multi-agent graphs, persist memory across sessions, connect to MCP servers natively, and isolate each merchant’s data automatically.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="why-amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Why Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore is a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale with any framework or model. Reactiv used three of its capabilities to address all four requirements. AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, handles managed agent execution. AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, provides persistent cross-session context. AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, handles service-to-service authentication.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Managed agent runtime&lt;/strong&gt;. AgentCore runs agents in Firecracker microVMs, the same isolation technology behind AWS Lambda. Reactiv packages their Strands agent graph as a Docker image, pushes it to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), and deploys it to AgentCore. Agents spin up when a schedule triggers and shut down when done. No Amazon ECS clusters, scaling policies, or idle compute.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;“We don’t manage containers, orchestrators, or scaling policies. We package our agent code as a Docker image, deploy it to AgentCore, and it handles the rest.” — Adam Gibicar, Senior AI Developer, Reactiv&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Built-in memory&lt;/strong&gt;. AgentCore provides long-term memory that persists across agent sessions. Reactiv uses three strategies. A session summarizer condenses each job’s actions into context for future runs. A preference learner tracks which layouts a merchant approves or rejects over time. A semantic fact extractor stores knowledge about the merchant’s store, such as product categories, top sellers, and brand guidelines. Memory is scoped per merchant, keeping each merchant’s data private. No custom vector database or retrieval pipeline required.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native MCP hosting&lt;/strong&gt;. Reactiv hosted their Config MCP on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. The MCP runs as a stateful server that initializes the merchant’s current app configuration at session start. The Builder Agent performs mutations against this live state, validated against the schema on every call. With AgentCore Identity, Reactiv handles service-to-service authentication natively, removing the custom authentication layer and JSON-RPC handshake code they had previously built and maintained.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-tenant isolation&lt;/strong&gt;. Each merchant’s execution context, memory, and agent state runs in its own Firecracker microVM. Merchant A’s preferences remain isolated from Merchant B’s sessions. AgentCore handles tenant routing and isolation at the infrastructure level.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the end-to-end flow of the AI Scheduler.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/18/ML-21280-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/18/ML-21280-1.png" alt="Reactiv AI Scheduler architecture: Amazon EventBridge triggers a Lambda that invokes AgentCore runtime with three Strands agents" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end flow of the Reactiv AI Scheduler. Amazon EventBridge triggers a Lambda executor that invokes the AgentCore runtime, where three Strands agents coordinate across MCP tools, Lambda functions, AgentCore memory, and Amazon Bedrock to produce app configurations stored in Amazon DynamoDB for merchant approval&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the services in the solution:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Role&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Managed agent runtime, persistent memory, MCP hosting, AG-UI support, multi-tenant isolation&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Foundation model access, guardrails&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Cron scheduling for merchant-defined update cadences&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Job executor and 17 tool functions invoked by agents&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon DynamoDB&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Result storage for merchant review and approval&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Redshift&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Analytics data store powering the Analytics Agent’s &lt;code&gt;text-to-SQL&lt;/code&gt; queries&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The system starts with the merchant. From the Reactiv dashboard, merchants create a scheduled task through either a chatbot interface (“Refresh my homepage with best sellers every Monday at 9 AM”) or a form where they pick a prompt, frequency, and time. Both produce a schedule record backed by an Amazon EventBridge cron rule.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When the schedule triggers, the flow proceeds through five components:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon EventBridge triggers a Job Executor (AWS Lambda) that validates the merchant’s account, acquires a concurrency lock, and pulls the merchant’s current app configuration from the database.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The Lambda function invokes the AgentCore runtime with the full context the agents need: the merchant’s prompt, current app configuration, and session metadata.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Inside the runtime, a Strands multi-agent graph executes:
  &lt;ul&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;The Supervisor Agent classifies the merchant’s intent and routes to the correct pipeline (analytics only, builder only, or analytics-then-builder).&lt;/li&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;The Analytics Agent queries merchant performance data through &lt;code&gt;text-to-SQL&lt;/code&gt; against Amazon Redshift, surfacing trends, top products, and actionable insights.&lt;/li&gt;
   &lt;li&gt;The Builder Agent takes those insights and produces updated app configurations. To do so, it calls over 50 tools. These include configuration mutations through the Config MCP, data queries through Lambda functions, product lookups from the Shopify Storefront SDK, and asset creation with an image generation SDK.&lt;/li&gt;
  &lt;/ul&gt;&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The Config MCP (hosted on AgentCore) validates every mutation the Builder Agent makes against Reactiv’s app schema. The Builder Agent cannot produce an invalid configuration because the MCP acts as both reference and guardrail.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The generated configuration is stored in Amazon DynamoDB for merchant review. Nothing goes live without explicit merchant approval.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Throughout execution, AgentCore memory reads and writes the merchant’s long-term memory in an isolated namespace. Every scheduled job builds on preferences and facts the agent accumulated in prior sessions for that specific merchant. The three agents access foundation models through Amazon Bedrock, which provides serverless inference and built-in guardrails without requiring Reactiv to manage model hosting or GPU infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="unifying-interactive-and-scheduled-agents"&gt;Unifying interactive and scheduled agents&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After launching the scheduler, Reactiv had two separate agent systems: the interactive dashboard agent (built with a custom UI adapter for Amazon Bedrock) and the scheduled agent (built on Strands with AgentCore). They did not share memory, tools, or infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reactiv unified them by migrating the interactive agent to the AG-UI protocol on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The dashboard agent now shares the same Strands framework, AgentCore-hosted MCP servers, and AgentCore memory instance as the scheduler. The result is one shared framework, runtime, memory layer, and UI protocol across both agents. The practical effect is bidirectional memory sharing. Preferences and facts learned during an interactive dashboard session feed directly into the next scheduled run, and vice versa. Scheduled jobs get smarter the more a merchant uses the front-end agent.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After moving to Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, Reactiv realized gains across development speed, runtime performance, and merchant experience. According to Reactiv’s internal measurements:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;80 percent reduction in merchant configuration time&lt;/strong&gt;. Onboarding tasks that took 17 hours of manual work now complete in 3 hours, and post-launch changes happen in minutes instead of days.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Simplified tooling and lower costs&lt;/strong&gt;. The Strands SDK &lt;code&gt;@tool&lt;/code&gt; decorator replaced approximately 100 OpenAPI spec files, AgentCore runtime and AgentCore Identity removed custom infrastructure, and the migration saves nearly $6,000 per year in compute costs alone.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Persistent memory with zero custom infrastructure&lt;/strong&gt;. Three long-term memory strategies per merchant, fully managed by AgentCore memory, with no vector database or retrieval pipeline to build or maintain.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;33 percent faster time to production&lt;/strong&gt;. A three-person team shipped the three-agent system in 10 weeks versus 15 weeks for the prior single-agent build. With AgentCore and Strands, the team focused engineering time on agent logic instead of infrastructure plumbing.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2x faster job execution&lt;/strong&gt;. Scheduled jobs dropped from over 10 minutes to approximately 5 minutes using native streaming in AgentCore runtime, which replaced a four-step polling chain with a single real-time invocation.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the interactive and scheduled agents unified on a single stack, Reactiv is extending the authoring surface available to merchants. The through-line: merchants can customize their mobile apps through natural language, in progressively deeper ways.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The first step is making Reactiv’s existing design properties (colors, typography, spacing, component variants) available to the agent as a new MCP server hosted on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. This mirrors the Config MCP approach: a shareable tool surface that the scheduler, the interactive builder, and eventually external integrations can all consume. Merchants can say “make my app match my brand” and the agent will apply changes within a governed design vocabulary rather than generating unconstrained output.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;That design system also unlocks a rebuilt onboarding experience. When a new merchant provides their brand name and website, the agent analyzes their existing web presence and produces a strong starting point for the mobile app. Because the design system is in place, the agent can target real design properties and components rather than generating layouts from nothing.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Further out, Reactiv plans to let merchants request entirely new layout sections through natural language. The agent would generate a validated JSON representation of the requested layout, which the mobile app renders on the fly using Reactiv’s design system components. This extends the agent’s authoring capability beyond pre-built section types into merchant-defined layouts that still follow brand guidelines.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Reactiv started with a single agent on self-managed infrastructure and grew into a unified multi-agent system on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Today their production system runs three specialized agents, over 50 tools, and persistent memory shared across interactive and autonomous modes. Multiple MCP servers run on one managed runtime. They delivered it faster, with less infrastructure code, and with capabilities that would have required months of custom engineering otherwise.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The MCP-based architecture has proven especially reusable. Reactiv’s Config MCP pattern now extends to a design system MCP and, eventually, to external developer tooling. Each new capability starts with defining tools, hosting them on AgentCore, and sharing them across agents.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;If you’re building agentic AI systems that need multi-tenant isolation, long-term memory, or managed MCP hosting, with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, you can access these through AgentCore runtime, AgentCore memory, and AgentCore Identity. To get started, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Right-size generative AI endpoints with concurrency sweeps on Amazon SageMaker AI</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/right-size-generative-ai-endpoints-with-concurrency-sweeps-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mona Mona]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 22 Sep 2026 15:35:53 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">de6c2facc757c64fbd0ea27218c6d35c1221274f</guid>

					<description>Concurrency sweeps help you right-size a generative AI endpoint on Amazon SageMaker AI by systematically benchmarking it at increasing load levels. This post walks through deploying a model, running automated concurrency sweeps with the CreateAIBenchmarkJob API, and using the results to make data-driven capacity decisions about fleet size.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Concurrency sweeps help you right-size a generative AI endpoint by finding the instance type and serving configuration that maximizes price-performance while holding latency within acceptable bounds. Without a systematic approach, right-sizing means deploying, load-testing manually, adjusting, and repeating until the numbers look acceptable. Choose five &lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instances when one would suffice, and you burn your budget on idle GPUs. Choose too few, and requests queue, latency spikes, and users experience degraded service.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Concurrency sweeps address this problem. A concurrency sweep is a systematic benchmarking approach that sends controlled, increasing levels of concurrent traffic to your Amazon SageMaker AI endpoint and analyzes its performance. Concurrency sweeps are built into &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/generative-ai-inference-recommendations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Inference Recommendations&lt;/a&gt;, so there’s no custom load-testing infrastructure to build or maintain.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through deploying the &lt;a href="https://huggingface.co/nvidia/NVIDIA-Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B-BF16" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA Nemotron-3 Nano 30B&lt;/a&gt; model, running automated concurrency sweeps, and using the results to make data-driven capacity decisions. By the end, you will know how many concurrent requests your endpoint can handle before latency becomes unacceptable, and how to automate that discovery.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="what-is-a-concurrency-sweep"&gt;What is a concurrency sweep?&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A concurrency sweep sends a controlled number of simultaneous requests to your SageMaker AI endpoint and measures two metrics at each level:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;Throughput&lt;/em&gt;:&amp;nbsp; how many tokens per second your endpoint produces.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;em&gt;Latency:&lt;/em&gt; how long each request takes.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;By progressively increasing the concurrency (for example, 64 to 256, and then 1,024 simultaneous requests), you can trace a curve that reveals your endpoint’s &lt;em&gt;saturation point&lt;/em&gt;. This is the point where adding more concurrent traffic stops improving throughput and degrades latency.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A concurrency sweep gives you three data points for production planning:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The ideal balance: the concurrency level where throughput is maximized with acceptable latency.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The breaking point: where latency crosses your service level agreement (SLA) threshold.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;The right-size factor: how many instances you need to cover your peak traffic, given the per-instance capacity.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Let’s now look at how the end-to-end workflow comes together.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The concurrency sweep workflow has four steps:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ol type="1"&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deploy&lt;/strong&gt; the model to a SageMaker AI endpoint using the native vLLM container.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Configure&lt;/strong&gt; the workload profile (input and output token counts, streaming mode).&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Run&lt;/strong&gt; the concurrency sweep using the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_CreateAIBenchmarkJob.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CreateAIBenchmarkJob&lt;/a&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Analyze&lt;/strong&gt; the results to identify optimal concurrency and right-size your fleet.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ol&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates this process.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 411px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21905-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21905-1.png" alt="Four-step concurrency sweep workflow: deploy the model, configure the workload profile, run the sweep, and analyze the results" width="401"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The four-step concurrency sweep workflow&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following four steps will take you from a fresh deployment to a complete capacity profile. Each step builds on the previous one, so we recommend following along with the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/03-features/gen-ai-inference-recommendations/inference_optimization_concurrency_sweep.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying notebook&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before getting started, make sure that you have:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with Amazon SageMaker AI access.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) execution role with permissions for SageMaker AI and Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3). You can check instructions in the notebook.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Service quota for &lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; endpoints.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-1-deploy-the-model-with-the-native-vllm-container"&gt;Step 1: Deploy the model with the native vLLM container&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We deploy NVIDIA Nemotron-3 Nano 30B, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with only 3B active parameters, to an &lt;code&gt;ml.g7e.2xlarge&lt;/code&gt; instance. This instance is backed by an &lt;a href="https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/products/workstations/professional-desktop-gpus/rtx-pro-6000-family/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVIDIA Blackwell GPU&lt;/a&gt;, which provides a strong price-performance ratio for inference workloads.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We use the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/reference/available_images/#vllm" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;vLLM Deep Learning Container&lt;/a&gt; for SageMaker AI, configured through &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_*&lt;/code&gt; environment variables:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;VLLM_IMAGE = (
    f"763104351884.dkr.ecr.{region}.amazonaws.com/"
    f"vllm:0.19.1-gpu-py312-cu129-ubuntu22.04-sagemaker"
)

environment = {
    "SM_VLLM_MODEL": "nvidia/NVIDIA-Nemotron-3-Nano-30B-A3B-BF16",
    "SM_VLLM_ENFORCE_EAGER": "true",  # required for Mamba-Transformer hybrid
    "SM_VLLM_TENSOR_PARALLEL_SIZE": "1",  # single GPU
    "SM_VLLM_GPU_MEMORY_UTILIZATION": "0.85",
    "SM_VLLM_MAX_MODEL_LEN": "10240",
    "SM_VLLM_ENABLE_PREFIX_CACHING": "true",
    "SM_VLLM_TRUST_REMOTE_CODE": "true",
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Three of these settings are worth explaining. The &lt;code&gt;SM_VLLM_ENFORCE_EAGER&lt;/code&gt; flag is required because Nemotron-3 Nano uses a Mamba-Transformer hybrid architecture that requires eager execution mode. We set the GPU memory utilization to 0.85 to leave headroom for KV cache growth under high concurrency. Prefix caching is enabled to improve performance in scenarios where system prompts are repeated across requests to benefit from reusing cached key-value pairs.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After creating the model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint, we verify the deployment with a validation before moving on to benchmarking.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-2-configure-the-workload-profile"&gt;Step 2: Configure the workload profile&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Before running any load test, the benchmark engine needs to know what kind of traffic to simulate. We define a workload profile that mirrors a realistic generative AI inference pattern using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_CreateAIWorkloadConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CreateAIWorkloadConfig&lt;/a&gt;. The following table summarizes the values we used for defining the workload. You can follow the code in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/03-features/gen-ai-inference-recommendations/inference_optimization_concurrency_sweep.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying notebook&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameter&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Value&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;tokenizer&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Model tokenizer ID&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Used to count tokens accurately&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;streaming&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;True&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Enables streaming responses (required for TTFT metrics)&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;prompt_input_tokens_mean&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1,024&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Average input prompt length&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;output_tokens_mean&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;256&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Average generated response length&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The choice of 1,024 input tokens and 256 output tokens is representative of Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) or summarization workloads. If your application uses shorter prompts and longer completions (such as code generation), adjust these values accordingly. Streaming is enabled to capture time to first token (TTFT) metrics, which are important for interactive user experiences where perceived responsiveness matters as much as raw throughput.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the workload profile defined, the next step is to launch the sweep.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-3-run-the-concurrency-sweep"&gt;Step 3: Run the concurrency sweep&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;We can now create the benchmark job with the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_CreateAIBenchmarkJob.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CreateAIBenchmarkJob&lt;/a&gt; API with the parameters to use in the sweep. In the scenario illustrated in the sample notebook, we have:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;sweep_params = {
    "concurrency": [64, 256, 1024],  # powers of 4
    "request_count": 1024,  # requests per concurrency level
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The benchmark engine (&lt;a href="https://docs.nvidia.com/aiperf/welcome-to-ai-perf-documentation" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AIPerf&lt;/a&gt;) runs each concurrency level sequentially within a single job, sending the total number of requests defined under &lt;code&gt;request_count&lt;/code&gt; at each level. Running the levels sequentially instead of in parallel means each measurement reflects a clean, isolated load. This approach gives you statistically stable estimates while keeping costs contained.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When the job completes, the results are written to the Amazon S3 path defined under &lt;code&gt;OutputConfig&lt;/code&gt; as a tarball containing per-level metrics in JSON format.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h3 id="step-4-analyze-the-results"&gt;Step 4: Analyze the results&lt;/h3&gt;
&lt;p&gt;After the job completes, we can download and parse the output for analysis. Four metrics matter most when reading the results:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput (output tokens/sec):&lt;/strong&gt; Does it plateau or keep climbing?&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p99 end-to-end latency&lt;/strong&gt;: Where does it cross your SLA?&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;p50 to p99 latency spread:&lt;/strong&gt; A widening gap signals queuing under load.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time to first token (TTFT)&lt;/strong&gt;: Critical for streaming user experiences.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;When you plot throughput against latency across the concurrency levels, the saturation point is visible as a “knee” in the curve: throughput flattens while p99 latency bends sharply upward. This happens at 256 concurrent requests in our test scenario. Concurrency levels below the knee are your safe operating region. Above it, the endpoint is overloaded and users are waiting.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21905-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21905-2.png" alt="Throughput-versus-latency curve with the saturation knee at 256 concurrent requests, where throughput flattens and p99 latency rises sharply" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Throughput and p99 latency across concurrency levels, with the saturation knee at 256 concurrent requests&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;At this point you have a complete picture of how your endpoint behaves under load. For many teams, this is sufficient to make a confident capacity decision. If you want to automate the search for the optimal operating point across model versions or instance types, the benchmark engine offers an automated alternative.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="going-further-automatic-sla-based-concurrency-search"&gt;Going further: Automatic SLA-based concurrency search&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The concurrency sweep in Step 3 requires you to choose the concurrency levels to test. You might not know the right range, or you might want to automate capacity planning across model versions. In either case, replace the fixed concurrency list with a search recipe in the same &lt;code&gt;CreateAIBenchmarkJob&lt;/code&gt; call. The &lt;code&gt;max-concurrency-under-sla&lt;/code&gt; recipe accepts one or more SLA thresholds and searches for the highest concurrency that satisfies all of them.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The following table describes the available SLA threshold parameters:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parameter&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Meaning&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Statistic&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requires streaming?&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;ttft_sla_ms&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Max Time To First Token (ms)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;p95&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;tpot_sla_ms&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Max Time Per Output Token (ms)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;p95&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;e2e_sla_ms&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Max end-to-end request latency (ms)&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;p99&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;error_rate_sla&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;Max fraction of failed requests&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;avg&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;For example, we can use the following search parameters to find the maximum concurrency where p99 end-to-end latency stays under 50 seconds:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;search_params = {
    "search_recipe": "max-concurrency-under-sla",
    "e2e_sla_ms": 50000,
    "concurrency_min": 16,
    "concurrency_max": 4096,
    "search_max_iterations": 10,
    "search_initial_points": 512,
    "request_count": 1024,
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The search engine uses an optimization planner that can converge on the answer in fewer iterations than a linear sweep. It starts with a broad range and narrows progressively, evaluating only the concurrency levels needed to identify the boundary. This can reduce both the number of iterations and the total cost of the search.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Iteration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concurrency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput (OTPS)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Passed SLA?&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;16&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;492.5&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;32&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;904.2&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;64&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1433.4&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;4&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;128&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2066.8&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;256&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2823.4&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;6&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;512&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2782.3&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✗&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;7&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;320&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2781.8&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;8&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;284&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2788.6&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✗&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this example, the planner starts at concurrency 16 and doubles through each iteration. At concurrency 512, the first SLA violation occurs, either because the p99 end-to-end latency exceeded the threshold or because invocations failed. The planner then narrows the search to the 256–512 range and finds that concurrency 320 meets the SLA at 2,782 tokens per second. The search runs for at most the number of iterations you define in &lt;code&gt;search_max_iterations&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="combining-multiple-slas"&gt;Combining multiple SLAs&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;A single SLA threshold is insufficient for most production workloads, as some scenarios require that a model must meet multiple SLAs at the same time. For instance, in interactive user experiences, you might need to control both the end-to-end latency and the time to first token. You can still use the &lt;code&gt;max-concurrency-under-sla&lt;/code&gt; search recipe by passing multiple SLAs under search parameters:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt;
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;search_params = {
    "search_recipe": "max-concurrency-under-sla",
    "e2e_sla_ms": 50000,  # p99 end-to-end &amp;lt; 50s
    "ttft_sla_ms": 1500,  # p95 time-to-first-token &amp;lt; 1.5s
    "concurrency_min": 16,
    "concurrency_max": 4096,
    "search_max_iterations": 10,
    "request_count": 1024,
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt;
 &lt;tbody&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Iteration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Concurrency&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput (OTPS)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Passed SLA?&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;16&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;480.2&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;32&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;888.6&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;3&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;64&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1429.9&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;4&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;128&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;2061.4&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✗&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
  &lt;tr&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;5&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;80&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;1599.3&lt;/td&gt;
   &lt;td&gt;✓&lt;/td&gt;
  &lt;/tr&gt;
 &lt;/tbody&gt;
&lt;/table&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this case, we observe that when putting SLAs in both the end-to-end latency and time to first token, the maximum level of concurrency supported is 80.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;With the benchmarking complete, let’s clean up the resources we created during this walkthrough.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="cleaning-up"&gt;Cleaning up&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_DeleteEndpoint.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;delete your SageMaker endpoints&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_DeleteEndpointConfig.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;endpoint configurations&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/APIReference/API_DeleteModel.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;models&lt;/a&gt; after testing. Endpoints bill by the hour whether or not they’re receiving traffic.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt;
&lt;p&gt;Concurrency sweeps replace guesswork with data in the capacity planning process for generative AI endpoints. Instead of over-provisioning as a precaution or discovering bottlenecks in production, you can systematically map your endpoint’s performance envelope. You can then make informed decisions about fleet size before a single user request hits your system.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to:&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;ul&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Deploy a model using the native vLLM container on Amazon SageMaker AI.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Run concurrency sweeps using the &lt;code&gt;CreateAIBenchmarkJob&lt;/code&gt; API.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Plot throughput against latency to identify the saturation point.&lt;/li&gt;
 &lt;li&gt;Use the &lt;code&gt;max-concurrency-under-sla&lt;/code&gt; recipe to automatically discover the optimal concurrency for your SLA targets.&lt;/li&gt;
&lt;/ul&gt;
&lt;p&gt;The complete notebook is available in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/03-features/gen-ai-inference-recommendations/inference_optimization_concurrency_sweep.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with your own models, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/generative-ai-inference-recommendations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Generative AI Inference Recommendations&lt;/a&gt; documentation. We have also created another &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sagemaker-genai-hosting-examples/blob/main/01-models/GLM/GLM-5.3-Flash/GLM-5.3-Flash.ipynb" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;example that benchmarks GLM 5.3 Flash on Amazon SageMaker AI using this feature&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
