		<title>The future of infrastructure resiliency starts with modernization</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-future-of-infrastructure-resiliency-starts-with-modernization/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-future-of-infrastructure-resiliency-starts-with-modernization/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Maria Bledsoe]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 10 Sep 2026 16:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Analytics]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Azure IaaS]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53814</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Modernization only succeeds when organizations have confidence that their infrastructure can withstand disruption and continue supporting critical operations.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/the-future-of-infrastructure-resiliency-starts-with-modernization/">The future of infrastructure resiliency starts with modernization</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="why-infrastructure-resiliency-is-essential-for-modern-applications-and-ai-workloads">Why infrastructure resiliency is essential for modern applications and AI workloads</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Organizations today face constant pressure to modernize; business-critical applications are being transformed, AI workloads are becoming foundational to business operations, and infrastructure environments continue to grow in complexity. Yet modernization only succeeds when organizations have confidence that their applications, data, and infrastructure can withstand disruption and continue supporting critical operations. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As organizations adopt distributed applications, AI-powered services, and hybrid and multicloud architectures, the resiliency of their IT estate has become more than a technical consideration, it is a business requirement. Resiliency is the ability of infrastructure and workloads to withstand, adapt to, and recover from disruptions while maintaining critical business operations. Organizations need infrastructure platforms that can help reduce the impact of disruption, maintain workload availability, and support effective recovery when challenges occur.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://info.microsoft.com/ww-landing-minimize-downtime-with-resilient-cloud-applications-wbnr.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn about resilient cloud applications</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At the same time, resiliency strategy is evolving. Historically, organizations often approached resiliency through backups, redundancy, and disaster recovery plans. While these capabilities remain essential, modern resiliency requires a broader approach that spans architecture, operations, recovery, and continuous optimization. Customers increasingly recognize that resiliency is not about preventing every disruption. It is about designing for uncertainty, minimizing operational impact, recovering effectively, and continuously strengthening readiness over time.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At Microsoft, we believe Azure IaaS resiliency is an ongoing partnership and shared responsibility that helps organizations modernize with confidence. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us" target="_blank">Microsoft Azure</a> provides the infrastructure foundation, platform capabilities, and guidance that enable customers to build resilience into workloads from the start, maintain operational continuity as environments evolve, and continuously improve recovery readiness over time.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="resilient-by-design">Resilient by design </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Resiliency starts long before an outage occurs.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As organizations modernize business-critical applications, cloud-native services, and AI workloads, resiliency can no longer be bolted on after deployment. The most effective resiliency strategies begin during planning and design, with architectures that align availability, recovery, performance, compliance, and operational requirements to the needs of each workload. Not every application requires the same resiliency strategy, and a one-size-fits-all approach is no longer sufficient. This is especially true for AI and business-critical workloads, where downtime, performance degradation, or data loss can have significant business consequences.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Azure helps organizations build resiliency into infrastructure from the start through availability zones, resilient networking architectures, durable storage options, recovery services, and proven guidance from the Azure Well-Architected Framework and Azure Architecture Center.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The recently announced <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-au/azure/resiliency/infrastructure-resiliency-manager-overview" target="_blank">Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a> extends this foundation by helping organizations define resiliency goals, understand workload criticality, identify gaps, and evaluate resiliency posture at the application level. Rather than relying on manual reviews and static assessments, organizations can continuously understand how workloads align to resiliency objectives and where improvements may be needed.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/resiliency/infrastructure-resiliency-manager-overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Explore Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">To further simplify resiliency adoption, Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager provides recommendations, deployment guidance, and AI-assisted experiences through the resiliency agent in <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/copilot" target="_blank">Azure Copilot</a>. Teams can describe workloads, generate resilient deployment templates, assess existing environments, and receive recommendations aligned to their resiliency goals. This helps organizations embed resiliency earlier in the lifecycle and reduce the effort required to operationalize best practices.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The goal is simple: make resiliency part of how applications are designed, not something organizations revisit only after a disruption has occurred.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="innovate-without-interruption">Innovate without interruption </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Modernization is not a one-time project. Applications evolve, new services are introduced, new dependencies emerge, and infrastructure environments continuously change.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As environments evolve, resiliency must evolve with them.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">One of the most common challenges organizations face is maintaining operational continuity while introducing change. New deployments, configuration drift, scaling requirements, infrastructure updates, and evolving application architectures can gradually move workloads away from their original resiliency objectives. What was resilient six months ago may no longer meet current availability or recovery requirements.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This is why resiliency is becoming a continuous operational practice rather than a one-time design exercise. Organizations increasingly need visibility into resiliency posture, the ability to prioritize remediation efforts, and mechanisms for validating whether workloads continue to meet business objectives as they grow and change. <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-gb/azure/resiliency/infrastructure-resiliency-manager-overview" target="_blank">Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a> helps organizations continuously assess resiliency posture, identify high-priority gaps, and increase uptime through recommendations, operational guidance, and application-centric resiliency management.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Azure is also embedding resiliency more deeply across the infrastructure stack, enabling the platform to respond to certain component-level disruptions while helping unaffected resources continue operating. This increasingly self-healing approach can reduce the blast radius of isolated failures and help maintain continuity as infrastructure conditions change. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Per-disk resiliency for <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/managed-disks-overview" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Managed Disks</a>, now available in public preview in select regions, illustrates this approach at the storage layer. Traditionally, when a virtual machine lost connectivity to an attached managed disk for an extended period, Azure recovered the virtual machine after connectivity was restored. With per-disk resiliency enabled, Azure can temporarily take only the affected data disk offline while allowing the virtual machine and its remaining disks to continue operating. After connectivity is restored, Azure automatically reattaches the disk.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For workloads that can tolerate the temporary loss of an individual data disk, including clustered applications, workloads using auxiliary disks, and certain containerized architectures, this approach can help reduce the impact of isolated storage disruptions and allow critical workload operations to continue. It reflects a broader trend in cloud resiliency: reducing the blast radius of failures and helping organizations continue innovating even when individual infrastructure components encounter issues.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/virtual-machines/disks-per-disk-resiliency" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Improve workload availability with per-disk resiliency</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="recover-with-confidence">Recover with confidence </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">No organization can prevent every disruption.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The measure of resiliency is not whether disruption occurs. It is how effectively organizations prepare for, respond to, recover from, and learn from those events.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Historically, recovery planning was often treated as a periodic exercise. Today, leading organizations recognize that recovery readiness must be continuously validated. Recovery plans that have never been tested may not perform as expected during an actual disruption.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Azure helps organizations improve recovery readiness through integrated backup, disaster recovery, monitoring, and resiliency management capabilities. Organizations can define recovery objectives, validate failover strategies, monitor recovery performance, and continuously improve resiliency posture over time. <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-gb/azure/resiliency/infrastructure-resiliency-manager-overview" target="_blank">Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a> and <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/chaos-studio" target="_blank">Azure Chaos Studio</a> extend this process by helping teams test recovery plans under controlled conditions, validate failover procedures, identify hidden dependencies, and measure recovery outcomes against defined objectives before a real disruption occurs.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A configuration that looks resilient on paper still has to withstand a real failure. Azure Chaos Studio helps organizations simulate outage conditions and validate how applications respond. From availability zone failures and database failovers to DNS and Microsoft Entra disruptions, teams can safely test assumptions, verify recovery procedures, and build confidence that their resiliency strategies will perform as intended. Guided drills, automated cleanup, and audit-ready reporting help transform resiliency validation into an ongoing operational practice rather than an infrequent event.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Recovery confidence also depends on protecting data and preparing for increasingly sophisticated cyber threats. Infrastructure failures are only part of the resiliency equation. Organizations must also plan for accidental deletion, data corruption, ransomware, and compromised credentials.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/backup" target="_blank">Azure Backup</a> helps organizations improve recovery readiness with built-in capabilities that protect backup data, support cyber resilience, and simplify recovery. Features such as immutable vaults, soft delete, multi-user authorization, and recovery orchestration help organizations preserve clean recovery points and restore critical workloads with confidence.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">When recovery involves a cyberattack rather than an infrastructure failure, trust becomes just as important as speed. Capabilities such as immutable vaults, multi-user authorization, and isolated recovery experiences help organizations identify trusted recovery points and restore operations without reintroducing compromised data or configurations.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The future of resiliency is not simply recovering faster. It is enabling organizations to build resilient foundations, operate with confidence as environments evolve, and continuously strengthen recovery readiness over time.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/azure-iaas/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Modernize confidently with help from the Azure IaaS Resource Center</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="see-azure-resiliency-capabilities-in-action">See Azure resiliency capabilities in action</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Join Microsoft&#8217;s <a href="https://info.microsoft.com/ww-landing-minimize-downtime-with-resilient-cloud-applications-wbnr.html" target="_blank"><strong>Azure webinar series</strong></a> <strong>&#8220;Minimize downtime with resilient cloud applications&#8221;</strong> episode on <strong>September 17</strong> at 10:00 AM PT, where Azure resiliency experts will demonstrate how organizations can build resilient architectures, assess resiliency posture, validate recovery readiness, and strengthen recovery outcomes using <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-gb/azure/resiliency/infrastructure-resiliency-manager-overview" target="_blank">Azure Infrastructure Resiliency Manager</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/backup" target="_blank">Azure Backup</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/site-recovery" target="_blank">Azure Site Recovery</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/chaos-studio" target="_blank">Azure Chaos Studio</a>, and the <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/copilot/resiliency-agent" target="_blank">Azure Copilot Resiliency Agent</a>.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Two zones or three? A design framework for zone-resilient Azure workloads</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/two-zones-or-three-a-design-framework-for-zone-resilient-azure-workloads/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/two-zones-or-three-a-design-framework-for-zone-resilient-azure-workloads/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Mark Russinovich, Eric Henry and Sai Vaidhyanathan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 09 Sep 2026 18:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Compute]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Migration]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Networking]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Storage]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Advancing reliability]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Azure Essentials]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53717</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Zone resiliency isn't a single number you apply to a whole workload. The useful question isn't “how many zones?” but “how many zones does each component need to survive the loss of one?” Decide zone patterns component by component, use service-managed zone redundancy wherever it fits, and reserve three-zone designs for the components that genuinely require a third failure domain.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/two-zones-or-three-a-design-framework-for-zone-resilient-azure-workloads/">Two zones or three? A design framework for zone-resilient Azure workloads</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><strong>Takeaway: </strong><em><em>Zone resiliency isn&#8217;t a single number you apply to a whole workload. The useful question isn&#8217;t “how many zones?” but “how many zones does each component need to survive the loss of one?” Decide zone patterns component by component, use service-managed zone redundancy wherever it fits, and reserve three-zone designs for the components that genuinely require a third failure domain. For a deeper, decision-oriented walkthrough on choosing between two-zone and three-zone patterns for your specific workloads, see the companion post, </em><a href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/reliability-and-resiliency-in-azure/choosing-two-zone-and-three-zone-patterns-for-zone-resilient-azure-workloads/4554527" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><em>Choosing two-zone and three-zone patterns for zone-resilient Azure workloads</em></a></em>.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Ask a room of architects how many Microsoft Azure availability zones a production workload needs, and most will answer “three.” It&#8217;s a reasonable instinct, but “three zones everywhere” is a rule of thumb standing in for a decision that deserves more thought. Treating it as a default quietly costs you capacity, money, and operational complexity you may not need while occasionally giving you less protection than you assumed.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Zone resiliency is not a property you switch on for an entire workload. It&#8217;s a set of decisions you make component by component. Some components are fully protected across two zones. Some genuinely need a third. And for a growing number, Azure manages zone redundancy for you, and the best decision is to let it. This post is about how to tell the difference.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/well-architected/design-guides/regions-availability-zones" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn architecture strategies for availability zones and regions</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="what-availability-zones-protect-against-and-what-they-don-t">What availability zones protect against (and what they don&#8217;t)</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">An Azure region that supports availability zones is made up of separate groups of datacenters, each with independent power, cooling, and networking. Zone resiliency protects you against the loss of one of those zones. It does not protect you against the loss of the entire region. If your workload is mission-critical or carries strict disaster-recovery requirements, zones are necessary but not sufficient: you also need a multi-region conversation, and that&#8217;s a separate design exercise.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">It&#8217;s worth being precise about what “using zones” means, because Azure services expose it in two different ways:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Zone-redundant resources</strong> are spread or replicated across zones by Azure itself. Azure handles the replication, request distribution, and failover. Where a service offers zone redundancy and it meets your requirements, it should generally be the starting point, especially for production workloads.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Zonal resources</strong> are pinned to one zone. A zonal resource is isolated from failures in other zones, but it is not automatically resilient to a failure in its own. To make a zonal design resilient, you deploy instances across zones yourself and own the routing, replication, failure detection, and recovery.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That distinction matters, because reliability on Azure is a shared responsibility. The resilience of a zone-redundant service is Microsoft&#8217;s responsibility to deliver; the resilience of a zonal design you assemble yourself is largely yours to configure and prove. No layer makes up for a gap in another.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="decide-at-the-component-level-not-the-workload-level">Decide at the component level, not the workload level</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The reason “three zones everywhere” may not be the correct starting point is that a workload is not one thing. It&#8217;s a stateless front end, an application tier, a queue, a cache, a relational database, an object store, and often a quorum-based system or two each with different failure behavior. Applying one zone count to all of them optimizes for none of them.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Break the workload down by critical flow, then by component, and ask three questions of each:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Resource availability: </strong>If one zone goes down, can the remaining zone or zones carry the operating state you need? For a single-zone failure, two zones and three zones can both meet the objective. The third zone does not, by itself, make a component resilient to more than one zone failure in the same region.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Data consistency and durability: </strong>Does the component&#8217;s correctness depend on a third failure domain? Quorum, consensus, leader election, split-brain prevention, and the highest durability targets often do.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Cost and capacity: </strong>For the same post-failure performance, recovery capacity is distributed differently across two zones versus three. Decide the resiliency objective first, then optimize cost, not the other way around.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">One subtlety worth flagging: If more than one zone is unavailable in the same region at once, your problem has usually stopped being a workload problem and become a regional one: foundational regional services themselves depend on surviving zones. Designing past a single-zone failure puts you in disaster-recovery territory, which is a different conversation.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="a-starting-point-where-common-components-tend-to-land">A starting point: Where common components tend to land</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Most components fall into one of a few buckets. Treat this as a first read, not a support matrix. Zone behavior varies by service, SKU, tier, region, and configuration, so always validate against the service&#8217;s own reliability guidance.</p>



<figure class="wp-block-table is-style-stripes"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><thead><tr><th>Component type</th><th>Typical zone decision</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td><strong>Stateless compute, application, or network components (no persistent data)</strong></td><td>Two or three zones, based on remaining capacity, routing, latency, and operations. Both can meet a single-zone objective.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Stateful systems using quorum, consensus, or leader election</strong></td><td>Three zones, a third failure domain, or a product-specific witness pattern to avoid split-brain and quorum loss.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Critical data stores needing three replicas for the highest durability targets</strong></td><td>Three-zone replication is often required to reach the intended durability level. Validate the service&#8217;s specific claims.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Other stateful resources</strong></td><td>Two-zone, three-zone, or service-managed, depending on recovery time objective (RTO), recovery point objective (RPO), durability, and failover behavior.</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Anything Azure can make zone-redundant for you</strong></td><td>Use service-managed zone redundancy when it meets your requirements.</td></tr></tbody></table></figure>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="where-two-zones-are-enough">Where two zones are enough</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Two zones is not a compromise. For many components it&#8217;s simply the right answer: fully meeting a single-zone failure objective while being simpler to deploy, test, and operate. Two zones tend to be enough when:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">The component is stateless or easily replaced, and traffic can shift to the surviving zone.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">An active-active or active-passive pair is easier to reason about and operate.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">A latency-sensitive synchronous path performs within budget on a tested zone pair.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">A customer-managed zonal design has validated capacity, failover, monitoring, recovery, and failback.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">The component can run in an approved degraded state after losing a zone.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  Whichever you choose, define exactly what happens when a zone goes away: remaining capacity, acceptable degradation, data consistency, failover behavior, recovery steps, observability, and who owns each of them.
</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="where-three-zones-earn-their-keep">Where three zones earn their keep</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Three zones are required when two cannot meet the component&#8217;s needs during or after a single-zone failure. In practice that&#8217;s usually one of:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">A post-failure operating state the remaining zone in a two-zone design simply can&#8217;t sustain</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Durability or replication that depends on placement across three zones: triple-replica placement for the highest durability targets</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Quorum, consensus, or leader-election designs that need a third failure domain or witness to avoid losing quorum or splitting brain.</li>
</ul>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A caution that trips up even experienced teams: three replicas are not the same as three failure domains. Place a majority-quorum system&#8217;s replicas across only two zones and you can still lose quorum when the majority-holding zone goes down. Replica count is not replica placement.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Be honest, too, about a middle category. Some components don&#8217;t require a third zone but benefit from one for capacity distribution, maintenance flexibility, or operational margin. That&#8217;s a legitimate reason to choose three zones. It&#8217;s just a different reason than &#8220;we&#8217;d lose the system otherwise,&#8221; and it&#8217;s worth writing down which one applies.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-cost-conversation-comes-last">The cost conversation comes last</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  Cost and operational complexity are real, but they belong at the end of the decision, not the start. Don&#8217;t let them talk you out of a two-zone design that meets requirements, and don&#8217;t let them justify a two-zone design that doesn&#8217;t. Counterintuitively, for the same post-failure capacity target, a three-zone design can provision less total capacity than a two-zone one, because the recovery headroom is spread across more zones. Model that tradeoff before you optimize, use commitment-based discounts such as <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/offers/savings-plans">Azure savings plans</a> or <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/cost-management-billing/reservations/save-compute-costs-reservations">Azure Reservations</a> where usage is predictable, and make sure whichever you pick can actually be operated: deployed, monitored, tested, failed over, recovered, and reviewed.
</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/offers/savings-plans" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Discover saving plans for compute and databases workloads</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="component-classification-checklist">Component classification checklist </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Run each component through this checklist and confirm that:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item">Every critical flow is decomposed into the components that support it.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Each component is evaluated for resource availability, data consistency and durability, and cost or capacity impact.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Each component is classified as service-managed zone-redundant, two-zone customer-managed, or three-zone required.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Wherever three zones are required, the specific requirement driving that decision is written down.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Each service&#8217;s zone support is validated for the SKU, tier, region, and configuration you&#8217;re actually using.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Customer-managed zonal resources have validated routing, load balancing, replication, failover, monitoring, recovery, and failback.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Remaining capacity and acceptable degradation after one zone loss are documented. </li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Quorum-based components have validated replica placement, witness or tie-breaker behavior, leader election, and split-brain prevention.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Latency-sensitive paths are tested across the actual zone placement they&#8217;ll run on.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Security, identity, monitoring, and data-protection requirements are preserved across the design.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item">Ownership for testing, incident response, failover, failback, and periodic reassessment are assigned.</li>
</ul>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="the-discipline-is-in-the-details">The discipline is in the details</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Zone resiliency isn&#8217;t a slider you set once for an entire workload, and it isn&#8217;t a number you can copy from someone else&#8217;s architecture. It&#8217;s a series of deliberate, component-level decisions about how much failure each part of your system has to absorb. The discipline is in making each one on purpose, writing down why, and validating that the design behaves the way you assumed when a zone actually goes down.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Get that right, and “two zones or three?” stops being a debate. It becomes what it should be: an answer you can defend, component by component.</p>

		<item>
		<title>Beyond the benchmark: How an adaptive approach drives scientific discovery</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/beyond-the-benchmark-how-an-adaptive-approach-drives-scientific-discovery/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/beyond-the-benchmark-how-an-adaptive-approach-drives-scientific-discovery/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aseem Datar]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Tue, 08 Sep 2026 20:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53782</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>For research and development (R&#38;D) organizations, the promise of agentic AI is not a better one-time answer. It is a new way to explore complex scientific and engineering problems: pursuing multiple hypotheses, validating them against evidence, learning from what does not work, and adapting their approach as new information becomes available.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/beyond-the-benchmark-how-an-adaptive-approach-drives-scientific-discovery/">Beyond the benchmark: How an adaptive approach drives scientific discovery</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  For research and development (R&amp;D) organizations, the promise of agentic AI is not a better one-time answer. It is a new way to explore complex scientific and engineering problems: pursuing multiple hypotheses, validating them against evidence, learning from what does not work, and adapting their approach as new information becomes available.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">This unique nature of the agentic discovery process has been a core area of research for Microsoft, and a design principle for <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/discovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Discovery</a>, our platform for organizations embracing Frontier R&amp;D. </p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoft-discovery-blog/adaptive-by-design-how-microsoft-discovery-explores-science/4552705" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Learn how Microsoft Discovery explores science</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="measuring-adaptive-ai-for-scientific-discovery">Measuring adaptive AI for scientific discovery</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A new benchmark result shows how that opportunity is becoming real. On <a href="https://agents-last-exam.org/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Agent’s Last Exam</a>, a demanding evaluation of long-running, tool-using professional tasks, <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/microsoft-discovery/concept-discovery-engine" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Discovery Engine</a> with CLIO (Cognitive Loop via In-Situ Optimization) <a href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoft-discovery-blog/adaptive-by-design-how-microsoft-discovery-explores-science/4552705" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">achieved higher scores than the other agentic harnesses evaluated across three scientific domains</a>: 61.6% in health and medicine, 75.2% in physical sciences, and 64.6% in life sciences.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  This result builds on Microsoft’s core research into what makes agentic discovery distinctive. CLIO enables independent reasoning paths to explore a problem, compare and share learning, and resolve the strongest trajectory into a single evidence-backed result. The system can determine when to keep exploring, change strategy, use a different model, or bring a domain expert into the loop.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The <a href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoft-discovery-blog/adaptive-by-design-how-microsoft-discovery-explores-science/4552705" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">CLIO benchmark blog post</a> describes this adaptive reasoning approach in depth. More broadly, this core innovation for scientific discovery, powered by agentic AI, is available to R&amp;D organizations in every industry and the scientific community with <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/discovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Discovery</a> not only as a research breakthrough, but as a foundation for real R&amp;D work.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/discovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Explore Microsoft Discovery</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="why-scientific-discovery-requires-adaptive-reasoning">Why scientific discovery requires adaptive reasoning</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  Many of the hardest scientific and engineering challenges do not have a clearly defined workflow or a known answer. A researcher may need to navigate incomplete evidence, competing objectives, specialized tools, and changing constraints. A materials team may be balancing performance, safety, cost, and manufacturability. A life sciences team may need to connect literature, proprietary data, models, and experimental evidence before deciding what to validate next. An engineering team may need to search a vast design space without sacrificing physical fidelity or traceability.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  In these settings, a single model response is not enough. Practitioners need systems that can reason over time, preserve evidence, challenge assumptions, and work within the tools, data, governance, and review processes their experts already use. Just as importantly, they need to understand how a conclusion was reached and where human judgment should enter the process.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/discovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Discovery</a> was designed as an enterprise platform for agentic R&amp;D, combining the scientific mindset of hypothesis, experimentation, and refinement with the engineering rigor of problem decomposition, structured execution, and reproducibility. CLIO strengthens that foundation with a more adaptive reasoning loop and a diverse model ecosystem, while allowing researchers to use a diverse model ecosystem and multiple reasoning paths.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://github.com/microsoft/discovery?hasfullconsent=true" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Try Microsoft Discovery app</a></div>
</div>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="from-benchmarks-to-real-world-impact">From benchmarks to real-world impact</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The greater opportunity extends beyond benchmark rankings into real research environments. Discovery Engine with CLIO has already supported work that discovered a <a href="https://nam06.safelinks.protection.outlook.com/?url=https%3A%2F%2Ftechcommunity.microsoft.com%2Fblog%2Fmicrosoft-discovery-blog%2Freinventing-organic-redox-flow-batteries-with-microsoft-discovery%2F4552343&amp;data=05%7C02%7Cv-estvirko%40microsoft.com%7C3471e9be37134bd29f0f08df0db0258a%7C72f988bf86f141af91ab2d7cd011db47%7C1%7C0%7C639244722368580525%7CUnknown%7CTWFpbGZsb3d8eyJFbXB0eU1hcGkiOnRydWUsIlYiOiIwLjAuMDAwMCIsIlAiOiJXaW4zMiIsIkFOIjoiTWFpbCIsIldUIjoyfQ%3D%3D%7C0%7C%7C%7C&amp;sdata=oDSHDxJtI%2BRHngUsPJvJLSfLa8Y5g9RAEQkWCx8DE10%3D&amp;reserved=0" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">novel organic redox flow battery</a>. The same approach has potential across design simulation (like for silicon chips), formulation and process optimization (for example in manufacturing and CPG), materials and molecular discovery (which can drive sustainability and drug discovery), and lab automation, areas where organizations need to shorten research cycles, without sacrificing rigor or traceability.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Agentic discovery does not replace scientists and engineers. It expands what they can explore, helps them learn faster from evidence, and gives them a more systematic and transparent way to move from an idea toward an outcome that experts can evaluate and validate.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Realizing the enormous opportunity to redefine R&amp;D requires a platform built for the tools, data, governance, and review processes researchers already use. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/solutions/discovery" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Discovery</a> was designed with that need in mind: to bring agentic discovery to researchers and scientists in R&amp;D organizations across every industry and throughout the scientific community. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  We are still early in this journey, but this benchmark milestone demonstrates what becomes possible when AI is built for the way discovery actually happens: iteratively, collaboratively, and adaptively. I look forward to seeing what organizations, researchers, and partners discover next.
</p>

<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="adaptive-ai-for-scientific-discovery">Adaptive AI for scientific discovery</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Learn how Microsoft Discovery uses adaptive, agentic approaches to explore complex scientific and engineering challenges, helping R&amp;D teams accelerate innovation and uncover new possibilities.</p>

<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/enterprise-ai-transformation-relies-on-the-end-to-end-platform-azure-was-built-for-this-moment/">Enterprise AI transformation relies on the end-to-end platform: Azure was built for this moment</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<div class="summary" data-bi-an="Summary Block" aria-describedby="summary">
	<p>
		<span class="summary__prefix">Summary</span>
		<span class="summary__content">The recognition for Microsoft over the past couple of weeks comes down to models, infrastructure, data, applications, and developer tools working as one system when AI moves into production.</span>
	</p>
</div>




<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Enterprise AI is moving into production, and our customers are becoming multi-model. Organizations will use frontier models where capability matters, and smaller, specialized, and open-weight models where economics and finer controls matter. But the value does not come from any model in isolation. It comes from the system around it: infrastructure, data, applications, agents, security, and operations working together. That compounding value is what <a href="http://azure.microsoft.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Azure</a> is built to deliver.  </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="a-system-built-from-silicon-to-agent">A system built from silicon to agent </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That integration extends into the infrastructure underneath the model. Customers want the flexibility to choose across models and infrastructure without having to stitch together and tune every layer themselves. Microsoft has drawn on decades of running mission-critical systems and operating some of the world&#8217;s most demanding AI services at global scale. We believe that breadth and integration across the platform, extending through developer tools and AI applications is a key reason why Microsoft has been named a Leader in both the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Strategic Cloud Platform Services and The Forrester Wave™: Public Cloud Platforms, Q3 2026.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We appreciate the recognition. What matters more is that customers choosing a platform today are shaping their infrastructure for years, and that choice rests on system-level capability. A cloud platform now must do more than provide individual services. It must give customers choice across models and infrastructure while helping them build faster, run reliably, manage risk, control cost, and improve outcomes. For an enterprise building the next generation of AI applications, how the layers work together matters more than any single feature.</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer noreferrer noopener">Discover trusted cloud solutions on Microsoft Azure</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft&#8217;s Leader placement in the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Strategic Cloud Platform Services follows Leader placements in the 2025, 2024, and 2023 editions. We believe that what matters for customers, is whether the platform can translate technology into real impact: better performance, greater cost efficiency, faster delivery, and the ability to scale critical systems with confidence.</p>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aa69158b84bc&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aa69158b84bc" class="wp-block-image aligncenter size-full wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Figure1-scaled.webp" alt="Gartner Magic Quadrant for Strategic Cloud Platform Services chart." class="wp-image-53734 webp-format" srcset="" data-orig-src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Figure1-scaled.webp"><button

<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft was also named a Leader in The Forrester Wave™: Public Cloud Platforms, Q3 2026. Forrester&#8217;s evaluation looks at both the strength of the current offering and the strategy behind it. This recognition provides another independent view of how <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/explore" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Azure</a> is evolving as customers move from isolated AI projects to production systems.</p>


<figure data-wp-context="{&quot;imageId&quot;:&quot;6aa69158b98e6&quot;}" data-wp-interactive="core/image" data-wp-key="6aa69158b98e6" class="wp-block-image size-large wp-lightbox-container"><img decoding="async" data-wp-class--hide="state.isContentHidden" data-wp-class--show="state.isContentVisible" data-wp-init="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on--click="actions.showLightbox" data-wp-on--load="callbacks.setButtonStyles" data-wp-on-window--resize="callbacks.setButtonStyles" src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-767x1024.webp" alt="Forrester Wave&trade; for Public Cloud Platforms, Q3 2026, showing Microsoft positioned among Leaders." class="wp-image-53705 webp-format" srcset="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-767x1024.webp 767w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-225x300.webp 225w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-768x1025.webp 768w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-1151x1536.webp 1151w, https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026.webp 1240w" data-orig-src="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/wp-content/uploads/2026/09/Image_Public-Cloud-Platforms-Q3-2026-767x1024.webp"><button

<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><em>Forrester describes Microsoft&#8217;s direction as a vision of Azure as a single, vertically integrated system.</em> </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="choice-without-complexity">Choice without complexity </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A multi-model strategy does not mean every model should run the same way. The platform must support those choices across heterogeneous compute while applying consistent security, identity, governance, reliability, and operations. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Foundry</a> is central to this approach. It gives developers broad model choice and the tools to evaluate, secure, monitor, and operate AI systems, with Azure infrastructure underneath. This is not about forcing every workload into one model. It is about using reducing the seams between layers so teams can make workload-specific choices while operating consistently across cloud, on-premises, edge, and third-party environments.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="data-gives-ai-its-business-value">Data gives AI its business value </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Model choice will keep changing, but the data and business context that make AI useful endure. Customers want to work with data where it already resides, without creating more copies or losing governance along the way. As Forrester puts it: &#8220;Models come and go; data has gravity.&#8221; </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoft-fabric" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Fabric</a> brings analytics and data together, and <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/business/microsoft-purview" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Microsoft Purview</a> applies governance across that estate. The Azure databases, including <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-sql/database" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure SQL</a> and <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/cosmos-db" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Cosmos DB</a>, connect AI to current operational data. On top of that foundation, <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/microsoft-iq" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft IQ</a> provides the unified enterprise intelligence layer, giving apps and agents consistent business context across work, data, and knowledge. Together, these capabilities let organizations change models without rebuilding the data, governance, and business context around every application.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en/customers/story/26599-unc-health-microsoft-fabric" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">UNC Health illustrates why that foundation matters</a>. By modernizing its analytics, the organization is creating a governed data environment that supports care, operations, and research within the requirements of a highly regulated industry. It is the kind of foundation organizations need before AI can be applied responsibly at scale.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="modernization-is-the-catalyst-to-ai">Modernization is the catalyst to AI </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The applications running a business today contain years of business logic, data, and operating knowledge. They need a modern home where they can continue to support proven processes and connect to new AI experiences. Modernization is therefore part of the AI work, not a separate project. Customers need to decide workload by workload whether to move it, update it, use a managed service, expose it to agents through secure interfaces, or rebuild the parts where there is a clear business reason.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Levi Strauss &amp; Co. shows how modernization and AI become part of the same journey. <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en/customers/story/26705-levi-strauss-and-company-azure-migrate" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">The company modernized its legacy infrastructure on Azure to build a more resilient foundation</a>, then used <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en/customers/story/26647-levi-strauss-and-co-microsoft-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Microsoft Foundry to introduce agents that simplify work and accelerate decision-making</a>. A heritage company did not have to leave its existing business behind to adopt AI; it modernized that foundation and built forward from it. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Agents can help teams assess applications, plan upgrades, refactor code, test changes, and support migration while developers and IT teams retain control of architecture and business decisions. GitHub Copilot agentic modernization supports .NET and Java applications, and the work connects across the software lifecycle. This is where the analyst feedback is especially relevant: Gartner highlights Microsoft&#8217;s pragmatic approach to application modernization and its integrated, end-to-end software developer lifecycle, while the Forrester report notes our customers’ appreciation for Microsoft&#8217;s migration and modernization expertise. The goal is straightforward: help customers modernize the applications they already rely on and so they are ready for the next generation of AI.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="power-every-ai-ambition">Power every AI ambition </h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">As customers run more AI in production, the platform must be more efficient, more reliable, and easier to operate. Customers need the freedom to choose the models and infrastructure that best fit each workload, while the platform reduces the complexity of bringing those choices together.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We’re proud to be recognized as a Leader by Gartner and Forrester, and even more excited by what these evaluations reflect about where the industry is heading. We believe the next generation of cloud will be defined by how well the platform brings infrastructure, data, models, applications, and developer tools together while preserving the choice customers need as each layer continues to evolve.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That’s the direction we’re building toward with <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Azure</a>, and we’re excited to keep shaping what comes next alongside our customers and partners.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Review the 2026 Gartner® Magic Quadrant™ for Strategic Cloud Platform Services. <strong><a href="https://www.gartner.com/reprints/?id=00ThR00000DWCzXUAX&amp;ct=260901&amp;st=sb" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Read the report</a></strong>.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Review The Forrester Wave™: Public Cloud Platforms, Q3 2026. <a href="https://reprint.forrester.com/reprints/the-forrester-wave-tm-public-cloud-platforms-q3-2026-382b8916" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Read the report</a>.</p>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>GPT-6 Astra, OpenAI's newest frontier model, begins rolling out today through the Microsoft Foundry Limited Access Program, with availability expanding to participating customers over the coming days.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/gpt-6-astra-frontier-intelligence-for-work-now-generally-available-in-microsoft-foundry/">GPT-6 Astra: Frontier intelligence for work, now generally available in Microsoft Foundry</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The next era of enterprise AI will not be defined by chat experiences. It will be defined by how well a model can work for and with you. <strong>GPT-6 Astra</strong>, OpenAI&#8217;s newest frontier model, is now generally available for all customers in <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/ai-foundry/" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Foundry</a>. It is designed to help organizations make decisions for complex work and execute across the applications and systems where your business operates.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">We are investing to make it easier for customers to use advanced technology like Astra. AI initiatives often slow down on identity, networking, governance, data handling, evaluation, and compliance. Microsoft Foundry brings these fundamentals together in Azure, helping teams move from experimentation to production with speed and trust.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="turn-open-ended-goals-into-action">Turn open-ended goals into action</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Astra is built to take an open-ended challenge, reason through it in multiple steps, create a plan, and produce a polished result. It can weigh trade-offs, incorporate new direction as work progresses, and use tools across applications and systems. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For enterprises, this shifts AI from conversational assistance toward delivering more substantial units of work:</p>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Deliberate planning and decision support.</strong> Astra can break a challenge into steps, evaluate options, communicate its recommendation, and identify the next actions for review.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Polished, purposeful output.</strong> Astra can apply context, templates, and quality standards throughout a workflow, helping produce documents, spreadsheets, presentations, and analyses that are ready for review.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Execution across applications.</strong> With advanced tool use and computer use, Astra can interact with software on a person’s behalf, move between apps, and complete multi-step tasks with appropriate human oversight.</li>
</ul>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At Replit, our mission is making useful intelligence accessible to everyone. GPT-6 Astra available through Microsoft Foundry unlocks a new level of agentic capability that goes beyond code generation to active software creation and more. We&#8217;re excited about the opportunities created for developers and entrepreneurs to build more ambitious applications with an intelligent software-building partner.</p>
<cite>—Luis Hector Chavez, CTO, Replit</cite></blockquote>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="computer-use-across-applications">Computer use across applications</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Astra’s computer-use capabilities are designed to work across familiar applications, including workflows without dedicated APIs. It can interpret on-screen information and interact with approved interfaces to support tasks such as updating records, navigating development tools, testing software, and assembling results into reports. OpenAI reports state-of-the-art results on selected computer-use evaluations; performance varies by task, tools, configuration, and safeguards.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Capability this direct demands containment. Content displayed in an application may be incomplete, misleading, or designed to influence an agent’s behavior. Foundry helps customers define access, approvals, and monitoring, and design workflows with scoped credentials, approved resources, human checkpoints for consequential actions, and activity records aligned to their risk requirements.</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="enterprise-scenarios-we-re-seeing">Enterprise scenarios we&#8217;re seeing</h2>



<ul class="wp-block-list">
<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Software engineering:</strong> Astra can reproduce complex bugs, investigate likely causes, propose fixes, and prepare changes for developer testing and review.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Business intelligence:</strong> Astra can build and refine dashboards in Power BI, helping analysts compare data, identify trade-offs, and prepare insights to share.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Professional work:</strong> Astra can produce documents, spreadsheets, and presentations that follow existing templates and business standards, creating polished artifacts for expert review.</li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><strong>Application workflows:</strong> Astra can support tasks such as updating customer records, processing forms, testing websites, and working through approved interfaces where dedicated APIs are limited. </li>
</ul>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="enterprise-controls-for-agentic-work">Enterprise controls for agentic work</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">OpenAI describes Astra as its most aligned model to date and plans to publish supporting alignment, safety, and computer-use evaluations in its supporting launch materials. Foundry complements that model-level work with enterprise security, safety, and compliance capabilities, including <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/concepts/authentication-authorization-foundry" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Microsoft Entra identity and access management</a>, encryption in transit and at rest, private networking options, role-based access controls, content filtering, safety evaluations, monitoring, and governance tools. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Prompts and outputs are not used to train the models. These capabilities help customers configure safeguards and maintain oversight, but do not eliminate risk or replace each organization&#8217;s responsibility to select and configure controls appropriate to its scenarios and regulatory obligations.</p>



<blockquote class="wp-block-quote has-quote-default-font-size is-layout-flow wp-block-quote-is-layout-flow">
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At Albertsons Companies, we believe the real advantage in frontier AI is the ability to evolve as quickly as the technology does, without compromising enterprise discipline. That means creating an environment where we can evaluate new capabilities, put the right ones to work quickly and maintain consistent security, governance and operational controls as we scale. Azure OpenAI on Microsoft Foundry helps us create that balance of speed and control, so our teams can stay focused on delivering meaningful outcomes for our customers, associates and the business.</p>
<cite>—Anirban Nandi, VP, Data and AI, Albertsons Companies</cite></blockquote>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="global-scale-with-service-level-to-match">Global scale with service level to match</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">GPT-6 Astra is available in Foundry models with Standard and Provisioned Throughput deployment options, in both Global and US Data Zone geographies. Standard provides pay-as-you-go flexibility for variable demand, while Provisioned Throughput provides dedicated model-processing capacity for workloads requiring consistent latency and guaranteed throughput. Customers can choose the right deployment based on workload requirements.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Astra is designed for token efficiency on complex work, helping customers manage consumption as they scale. Actual usage and costs will vary by workload and configuration.</p>



<h3 class="wp-block-heading" id="gpt-6-astra-pricing">GPT-6 Astra pricing*</h3>



<figure class="wp-block-table"><table class="has-fixed-layout"><tbody><tr><td rowspan="2">Deployment </td><td rowspan="2">Context Length   </td><td colspan="4">Pricing (USD $/million tokens) </td></tr><tr><td>Input </td><td>Cached Input </td><td>Cached Writes </td><td>Output </td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2">Standard  Global </td><td>Short context </td><td>$10.00 </td><td>$1.00 </td><td>$12.50 </td><td>$50.00 </td></tr><tr><td>Long context </td><td>$20.00 </td><td>$2.00 </td><td>$25.00 </td><td>$75.00 </td></tr><tr><td rowspan="2">Standard  Data Zone (US)  </td><td>Short context </td><td>$11.00 </td><td>$1.10 </td><td>$13.75 </td><td>$55.00 </td></tr><tr><td>Long context </td><td>$22.00 </td><td>$2.20 </td><td>$27.50 </td><td>$82.50 </td></tr></tbody></table><figcaption class="wp-element-caption"><em>Provisioned Throughput pricing varies by deployment type. U.S. Data Zone Provisioned Throughput is priced at a 10% premium to Global Provisioned Throughput. For current rates and terms, see the </em><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/details/azure-openai/?msockid=0030611da9e260e628f5742ca8586188" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener"><em>Azure OpenAI pricing page</em></a><em>.</em></figcaption></figure>

		<item>
		<title>How Microsoft&#8217;s Physical Security Engineering Team scaled hybrid operations with Azure Arc and Azure Virtual Desktop</title>
		<link>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/how-microsofts-physical-security-engineering-team-scaled-hybrid-operations-with-azure-arc-and-azure-virtual-desktop/</link>
					<comments>https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/how-microsofts-physical-security-engineering-team-scaled-hybrid-operations-with-azure-arc-and-azure-virtual-desktop/#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Brent Halazon]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 03 Sep 2026 15:00:00 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Compute]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[DevOps]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Hybrid + multicloud]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Management and governance]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Datacenter]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=53653</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Learn how Microsoft used Azure Arc and Azure Virtual Desktop to simplify hybrid security operations, improve visibility, and scale globally.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/how-microsofts-physical-security-engineering-team-scaled-hybrid-operations-with-azure-arc-and-azure-virtual-desktop/">How Microsoft&#8217;s Physical Security Engineering Team scaled hybrid operations with Azure Arc and Azure Virtual Desktop</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph" id="when-a-physical-security-operator-begins-a-shift-supporting-microsoft-s-global-datacenter-operations-they-depend-on-a-collection-of-applications-and-systems-that-help-monitor-access-activity-review-video-feeds-investigate-alerts-and-coordinate-physical-security-operations-across-a-complex-global-environment-those-tools-must-be-available-responsive-and-reliable-from-the-moment-a-shift-begins">When a physical security operator begins a shift supporting Microsoft&#8217;s global datacenter operations, they depend on a collection of applications and systems that help monitor access activity, review video feeds, investigate alerts, and coordinate physical security operations across a complex global environment. Those tools must be available, responsive, and reliable from the moment a shift begins. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  As Azure datacenters expanded to support growing demand for cloud and AI services, maintaining that experience became increasingly important. Critical security systems were distributed across hundreds of locations worldwide, while the infrastructure supporting them spanned both on-premises and cloud environments. The challenge wasn&#8217;t responding to a specific incident or operational failure but ensuring that as Azure&#8217;s physical footprint continued to grow, the systems supporting those operations remained secure, manageable, observable, and consistent at a global scale.
</p>



<div class="wp-block-buttons is-content-justification-center is-layout-flex wp-container-core-buttons-is-layout-a89b3969 wp-block-buttons-is-layout-flex">
<div class="wp-block-button"><a class="wp-block-button__link wp-element-button" href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-arc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">See how Azure Arc unifies hybrid operations</a></div>
</div>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Meeting that goal required more than simply keeping systems online. The team needed a way to manage infrastructure across hybrid environments, standardize operations, automate routine tasks, improve visibility into system health, and provide operators with consistent application experiences regardless of location. By combining <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-arc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Arc</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/virtual-desktop" id="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/virtual-desktop" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Virtual Desktop</a>, <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/monitor" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Monitor</a>, and other Azure management services, Microsoft built a more unified operational foundation designed to support the evolving needs of its global physical security environment. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="building-a-unified-management-layer-across-hybrid-infrastructure">Building a unified management layer across hybrid infrastructure</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  As Azure datacenters expanded, so did the infrastructure supporting their physical security operations. Critical systems were deployed close to the environments they served and operated within highly segmented networks designed to prioritize resiliency, security, compliance, and local autonomy. That architecture solved one challenge but created another.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  The physical security organization was responsible for deploying and managing thousands of servers distributed across Microsoft&#8217;s global datacenter footprint in alignment with established protocols. While each deployment met baseline operational requirements, rapid growth and increasing scale made it increasingly difficult to guarantee consistency.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  The team needed a way to bring these distributed systems under a common management framework without changing where the workloads ran or weakening the security boundaries that protected them.
</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="why-azure-arc">Why Azure Arc</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  The objective wasn&#8217;t to move these workloads into Azure. Many of the systems supporting physical security operations needed to remain close to the environments they served and continue functioning independently when required by local operational or resiliency needs. Instead, the team was looking for a way to extend the operational benefits of Azure to on-premises infrastructure.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-arc" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Arc</a> was designed to address exactly this type of challenge. At its core, Azure Arc extends Azure&#8217;s management and governance capabilities to servers and resources running outside Azure. Rather than treating on-premises systems as separate operational islands with their own tools and processes, Azure Arc allows organizations to manage those resources through Azure&#8217;s control plane. This makes it possible to apply, at scale, many of the same monitoring, policy, automation, security, and update-management workflows used in Azure to infrastructure running elsewhere. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  For Microsoft&#8217;s physical security organization, Azure Arc made it possible to manage servers across its global datacenter footprint through a common operational model, regardless of where they were physically located.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  More importantly, Azure Arc allowed the team to preserve the resiliency and security characteristics of their existing deployments while gaining centralized visibility, governance, and automation capabilities.
</p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="establishing-a-consistent-operational-foundation">Establishing a consistent operational foundation</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Once onboarded to Azure Arc, the team began extending familiar Azure management capabilities to infrastructure running outside Azure. Using <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-update-management-center" id="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/azure-update-management-center" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Update Manager</a>, patching activities that had historically required significant coordination across distributed environments could be scheduled, tracked, and governed through a centralized framework. According to the team, this automation now saves thousands of hours annually while enabling a relatively small operations team to support a growing infrastructure footprint. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">At the same time, Azure Policy, Guest Configuration, Azure Monitor, Azure Copilot Observability Agent, and Log Analytics helped create a common framework for governance, compliance monitoring, and observability. The team could continuously assess critical security configurations, identify drift, monitor system health, and surface operational telemetry through centralized dashboards, alerts, and reporting workflows regardless of where infrastructure was deployed.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Security remained a primary consideration throughout the design. Managed Identities and Azure role-based access control (RBAC) helped reduce reliance on stored credentials while providing more granular control over access to operational resources. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/automation" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Automation</a> further reduced manual effort by standardizing remediation, maintenance, and configuration-management activities through reusable runbooks. Together, these capabilities helped establish a more consistent operating model across the environment while improving visibility, strengthening governance, and reducing the operational overhead associated with managing a globally distributed infrastructure. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="delivering-a-consistent-operator-experience-with-azure-virtual-desktop">Delivering a consistent operator experience with Azure Virtual Desktop</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  Unified management solved one part of the challenge. The next was ensuring that operators interacting with those systems received the same level of consistency, performance, and visibility.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The team&#8217;s objective extended beyond providing remote access. They needed a way to improve application performance, simplify lifecycle management, and gain better insight into the end-user experience. <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/products/virtual-desktop" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Azure Virtual Desktop</a> provided a flexible platform for delivering applications closer to the infrastructure they depended on, while also enabling centralized image management and integration with Azure monitoring services. This allowed the team to maintain consistent host configurations, simplify updates, and incorporate user-session telemetry into existing operational workflows. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">To improve the operator experience, the team relocated the application environment closer to the infrastructure it supported and delivered access through Azure Virtual Desktop sessions. The impact was immediate: application launch times improved by approximately 12x, helping operators access critical tools more quickly and consistently. </p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  The team also adopted a centralized image-management strategy and automated host refresh process. Instead of maintaining individual systems over time, hosts could be rebuilt from approved images and deployed consistently across the environment. This approach accelerated release cycles by ~6x, reduced configuration drift, and allowed updates that once required weeks or months of coordination to be completed in hours.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Equally important was the visibility Azure Virtual Desktop unlocked. By integrating Azure Virtual Desktop with Azure Monitor, Azure Copilot Observability Agent, Log Analytics, and Azure Virtual Desktop Insights, the team gained access to telemetry on session health, round-trip time, bandwidth usage, and client-side application behavior. Engineers could better understand how applications performed from the operator&#8217;s perspective, identify trends earlier, and shift from reactive troubleshooting to a more proactive, data-informed approach. </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="key-lessons-for-managing-hybrid-environments-at-scale">Key lessons for managing hybrid environments at scale</h2>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">
  As Azure&#8217;s global datacenter footprint continued to grow, Microsoft&#8217;s physical security organization needed a management and delivery model that could scale alongside it. By combining Azure Arc and Azure Virtual Desktop, the team established a more consistent approach to managing infrastructure, delivering applications, and monitoring operational health across a complex hybrid environment.
</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The result wasn&#8217;t a single breakthrough technology, but a unified operating model that improved visibility, reduced operational overhead, and helped ensure critical systems remained resilient, manageable, and ready to support future growth.  </p>



<h2 class="wp-block-heading" id="learn-more">Learn more</h2>

<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/products/azure-arc/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Azure Arc</a></li>



<li class="wp-block-list-item"><a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/products/virtual-desktop/" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">Azure Virtual Desktop</a></li>

</ul>
